 \qquad M/B^{0.14}\asymp B^{0.18}.$$ The original full-sample event $\Omega_{\rm deg}$ implies $\mathcal G_{R'}$ for every subset $J'$ simultaneously, by nonnegativity of the envelope. Consequently $$\mathbb P\left(\Omega_{\rm deg}\ \text{and some assignment has}
          \ Z_{J',\sigma,\mathbf S_{J'}}>E_0+\epsilon_2/2\right)
 \le N_B\exp(-c_{\epsilon_2}M/B^{0.14})=o(1).$$ Only the exponential extension tails have been union-bounded; the probability of $\Omega_{\rm deg}^c$ is counted once.

With probability $1-o(1)$, the squared-mass event also holds, and Lemma <a href="#lem:sampling-skeleton" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:sampling-skeleton">35</a> now bounds the fully adaptive cut norm by $E_0+\epsilon_2/2+O(B^{-0.01})+O(B^{-0.11})$. On the exceptional event use $\|\mathcal E\|_\square\le A_B$. Lemma <a href="#lem:sampling-good" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:sampling-good">34</a> makes its expected contribution $o(1)$. This proves <a href="#eq:45" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:45">[eq:45]</a>. All probabilities and errors used here were uniform for each fixed $s$ in the compact range, which proves the stated uniformity without constructing a simultaneous event over $s$. ◻

</details>

The deterministic row estimate <a href="#eq:kernel-main-row-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kernel-main-row-bounds">[eq:kernel-main-row-bounds]</a> gives $$\sum_{i,k}|\mathcal M_{ik}|
 \ll_{\eta,m_1}M=O(B^{0.32}),$$ uniformly in the site types and in $s$. Thus $\mathcal M$ satisfies the $O(B^{10})$ total-mass hypothesis of the independent-root comparison.

Finally, combine <a href="#eq:45" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:45">[eq:45]</a> with the independent-site comparison <a href="#eq:21" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:21">[eq:21]</a>. For the actual sets $S_i(n)=\{p\in\mathcal P:p\mid n+i\}$ and fixed $B$, the upper limit as $x\to\infty$ of the absolute error in replacing the kernel in <a href="#eq:19" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:19">[eq:19]</a> by $\mathcal M_{ik}(S_i(n),S_k(n);n/(Tx))$ is at most $$C_{\rm lab}(E_0+\epsilon_2)+o(1).$$ Here $x$ tends along the fixed bad subsequence, and the $o(1)$ tends to zero as $B\to\infty$ after this inner upper limit. The constant $C_{\rm lab}$ depends only on the fixed label bound and the compact $s$-range. Indeed bounded complex label energies are controlled by a fixed multiple of the real cut norm. For each fixed $B$, the error norm is a finite maximum of finite-residue functions continuous in $s$ (piecewise continuity would also suffice), so ordinary scale counting gives its Haar expectation integrated over $s$, as explained after <a href="#eq:19" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:19">[eq:19]</a>. The uniform expectation bound above can then be integrated over that fixed compact interval. This uses no independence between the actual labels and site types, and all matrices retain zero entries on nonallowed pairs.

<a id="sec:conclusion"></a>
### Vanishing of the main energy

The main kernel in <a href="#eq:43" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:43">[eq:43]</a> has finitely many bounded site features. We first approximate these features by multiplicative weights and the lag factor by a function with a fixed period. Subdividing the position block then reduces its energy to products of the short averages in Lemma <a href="#lem:labels-short" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:labels-short">5</a>. In the vanishing argument, the regularity parameters, $C_*$, $\eta$, $C_6$, and the coarse partition are fixed; further approximation parameters are chosen before the limits. We take $x\to\infty$ along the bad subsequence fixed in Section <a href="#sec:labels" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:labels">2</a>, and only then $B\to\infty$.

<a id="eq:conclusion-main-energy"></a>
Choose a compact interval $\mathcal I_s=[s_0,s_1]\subset(0,\infty)$ containing the supports in $s$ of all the kernels under consideration. It depends only on the fixed smooth functions: the support conditions $\psi(u_1)\psi(u_2)\ne0$ and $\phi(s/u_1)\phi(s/u_2)\ne0$ put $s$ in such a fixed interval. For the actual site sets $$S_i(n)=\{p\in\mathcal P:p\mid n+i\},$$ write the main-kernel energy as $$\mathcal Q^{\mathrm{main}}_{B,x}
 =\frac1{Tx}\sum_{n:\,n/(Tx)\in\mathcal I_s}\frac1M
   \sum_{\substack{1\le i,k\le M\\\eta T\le |k-i|\le T}}
       \overline{F_x(n+i)}F_x(n+k)
       \mathcal M_{ik}(S_i(n),S_k(n);n/(Tx)).
 \label{eq:conclusion-main-energy}$$ The summands are zero away from the original smooth supports. In particular, the choice of the enclosing interval introduces no new term.

<a id="stmt-lem-conclusion-features"></a>
<div id="lem:conclusion-features" class="lemma">

**Lemma 37** (Approximation of the site features). *Fix the coarse partition $I=\bigcup_{l=1}^{m_1}I_l$ used in <a href="#eq:43" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:43">[eq:43]</a>. For every $\zeta>0$ there are real polynomials $$p_l(t)=\sum_{v=0}^{d_l}\alpha_{l,v}t^v,
 \qquad 1\le l\le m_1,$$ independent of $B$, such that the functions $$\widetilde V_{l,B}(S)
   =\sum_{v=0}^{d_l}\alpha_{l,v}
       \prod_{p\in S}\frac{1+p^{-v/B}}2$$ satisfy, for all sufficiently large $B$, $$\|V_l-\widetilde V_{l,B}\|_{L^2(S)}\le\zeta.$$ Here the norm uses the independent site law with inclusion probabilities $1/p$. For $0<\zeta\le1$, the approximants can be chosen with a common bound depending only on the fixed coarse partition. At actual integer sites they are the fixed finite combinations $$\widetilde V_{l,B}(S_i(n))
       =\sum_{v=0}^{d_l}\alpha_{l,v}G_{B,v}(n+i),$$ where $G_{B,v}$ is the function in <a href="#eq:labels-G" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:labels-G">[eq:labels-G]</a> with $P_B=\mathcal P$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-conclusion-features"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-conclusion-features</summary>

*Proof.* Write $x_b=(\log b)/B$ for the normalized logarithm of the fair split product $b$, and put $q_l^0(y)=\mathbf 1_{I_l}(y)/|I_l|$ for $y\ge0$. The endpoints of the coarse cells lie in $[1/2,3]$, where <a href="#eq:2" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:2">[eq:2]</a> bounds the marginal split-product densities uniformly for large $B$. Choose small endpoint neighborhoods, still in a fixed compact subinterval of $(0,3.2)$, and replace $q_l^0$ by a continuous compactly supported function $q_l$. We may arrange $0\le q_l\le1/|I_l|$, with equality to $q_l^0$ outside these neighborhoods. By <a href="#eq:2" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:2">[eq:2]</a>, their $\nu_{1/2}$ mass is at most a constant times their total length plus $O(B^{-80})$. Consequently the neighborhoods can be fixed so that $$\limsup_{B\to\infty}
   \mathbb E_{\nu_{1/2}}|q_l^0(x_b)-q_l(x_b)|^2$$ is as small as desired, simultaneously for the finitely many cells.

The function $t\mapsto q_l(-\log t)$ on $(0,1]$, assigned value zero at $t=0$, is continuous on $[0,1]$. Indeed $q_l$ is zero for all sufficiently large arguments. Uniform polynomial approximation on $[0,1]$ therefore gives a real polynomial $p_l$ for which $p_l(e^{-y})$ approximates $q_l(y)$ uniformly for every $y\ge0$. Both this approximation and the preceding endpoint smoothing can be chosen so that $$\bigl\|q_l^0(x_b)-p_l(e^{-x_b})\bigr\|_{L^2(\nu_{1/2})}
 \le\zeta$$ for all sufficiently large $B$. Taking the uniform polynomial error at most one also ensures $\sup_{0\le t\le1}|p_l(t)|\le1/|I_l|+1$.

Conditional on a site set $S$, fair splitting gives $$V_l(S)=\mathbb E_{\mathrm{split}}[q_l^0(x_b)\mid S],
 \qquad
 \mathbb E_{\mathrm{split}}[e^{-v x_b}\mid S]
      =\prod_{p\in S}\left(\frac12+\frac12p^{-v/B}\right).$$ The second identity follows by making the fair split choices separately for each prime. Conditional expectation is a contraction in $L^2$, which proves the asserted error bound and the uniform bound on the approximants. The last identity applies to the distinct primes dividing $n+i$, irrespective of their multiplicities, and hence gives exactly $G_{B,v}(n+i)$ at the integer sites. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:conclusion-feature-counting"></a>
We spell out how the feature error is used against the labels. For fixed $B$, any function of $S_i(n)$ is a function of finitely many residues of $n+i$. Unweighted progression counting gives, for $r=1,2$, $$\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac1{Tx}
    \sum_{n:\,n/(Tx)\in\mathcal I_s}
       |V_l(S_i(n))-\widetilde V_{l,B}(S_i(n))|^r
 =|\mathcal I_s|\,
       \mathbb E|V_l(S)-\widetilde V_{l,B}(S)|^r.
 \label{eq:conclusion-feature-counting}$$ There are only finitely many $i$ at fixed $B$; neither a bound on the size of the residue modulus nor uniformity with respect to growing $B$ is required for this inner limit.

<a id="eq:conclusion-lag-rows"></a>
The singular series in <a href="#eq:41" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:41">[eq:41]</a> is nonnegative and at most $j/\varphi(j)$. Its ordinary mean is bounded. Thus the lag majorant has deterministic row bounds $$\sup_{1\le i\le M}
   \frac1T\sum_{\substack{1\le k\le M\\0<|k-i|\le T}}
      \mathfrak S(|k-i|)\ll1.
 \label{eq:conclusion-lag-rows}$$ For completeness, the identity $$\frac{j}{\varphi(j)}
   =\sum_{d\mid j}\frac{\mu_{\mathrm{Mob}}^2(d)}{\varphi(d)}$$ and the convergence of $\sum_d\mu_{\mathrm{Mob}}^2(d)/(d\varphi(d))$ give this bound by summing the divisor expansion up to $T$. Since $|F_x|\le2$, the features and their approximants are bounded, and $k_B$, $w$ and the finitely many $h_l$ are bounded for the present fixed parameters, replacing both features in each term of <a href="#eq:conclusion-main-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:conclusion-main-energy">[eq:conclusion-main-energy]</a> costs at most $C\zeta$ in iterated upper limit. Indeed $$|V_l(S_i)V_l(S_k)
       -\widetilde V_{l,B}(S_i)\widetilde V_{l,B}(S_k)|
 \le C_{m_1}\bigl(
      |V_l(S_i)-\widetilde V_{l,B}(S_i)|
     +|V_l(S_k)-\widetilde V_{l,B}(S_k)|\bigr),$$ and <a href="#eq:conclusion-lag-rows" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:conclusion-lag-rows">[eq:conclusion-lag-rows]</a> reduces the sum to the single-site $L^1$ errors in <a href="#eq:conclusion-feature-counting" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:conclusion-feature-counting">[eq:conclusion-feature-counting]</a>. This uses no independence between either feature error and the labels or the other endpoint.

<a id="stmt-lem-conclusion-periodic"></a>
<div id="lem:conclusion-periodic" class="lemma">

**Lemma 38** (Periodic approximation of the lag factor). *For a fixed prime cutoff $P_1\ge2$, define on all integers $j$ $$\mathfrak S_{P_1}(j)
 =\prod_{\substack{p\le P_1\\p\mid j}}(1-1/p)^{-1}
   \prod_{\substack{p\le P_1\\p\nmid j}}
       \left(1-\frac1{(p-1)^2}\right).$$ This is a bounded nonnegative function of period $D=\prod_{p\le P_1}p$. Moreover, $$\lim_{P_1\to\infty}\ 
   \sup_{\substack{T\in\mathbb N\\T\ge1}}\frac1T
       \sum_{1\le j\le T}
         |\mathfrak S(j)-\mathfrak S_{P_1}(j)|=0,$$ with the supremum taken over positive integers $T$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-conclusion-periodic"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-conclusion-periodic</summary>

*Proof.* Write $$a(j)=\prod_{p\mid j}(1-1/p)^{-1},\qquad
 a_{P_1}(j)=\prod_{\substack{p\le P_1\\p\mid j}}(1-1/p)^{-1}.$$ The omitted nondividing-prime factors form a product of numbers in $(0,1]$. Since $P_1\ge2$, its difference from one is at most $$\sum_{p>P_1}\frac1{(p-1)^2}\ll P_1^{-1},$$ uniformly in $j$. All the retained nondividing-prime factors are in $[0,1]$, including the possible zero factor at $p=2$. Consequently $$|\mathfrak S(j)-\mathfrak S_{P_1}(j)|
 \le a(j)-a_{P_1}(j)+C P_1^{-1}a(j).$$ The positive divisor expansion gives $$a(j)-a_{P_1}(j)
  =\sum_{\substack{d\mid j\\
                   d\text{ has a prime divisor greater than }P_1}}
      \frac{\mu_{\mathrm{Mob}}^2(d)}{\varphi(d)}.$$ Its mean up to $T$ is bounded by $$\sum_{\substack{d\ge1\\
                   d\text{ has a prime divisor greater than }P_1}}
     \frac{\mu_{\mathrm{Mob}}^2(d)}{d\varphi(d)}.$$ The unrestricted positive series equals $\prod_p(1+1/(p(p-1)))<\infty$, so this tail tends to zero. The same expansion bounds the mean of $a(j)$ by that product, uniformly in $T$. The required conclusion follows. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-prop-conclusion-main-vanishes"></a>
<div id="prop:conclusion-main-vanishes" class="proposition">

**Proposition 39** (Vanishing of the main energy). *With $C_*$, $\eta$, $C_6$, and the coarse partition fixed as above, $$\lim_{B\to\infty}\ \limsup_{x\to\infty}
                   |\mathcal Q^{\mathrm{main}}_{B,x}|=0.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-conclusion-main-vanishes"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-conclusion-main-vanishes</summary>

*Proof.* Let $\gamma>0$ be arbitrary. First choose the approximants in Lemma <a href="#lem:conclusion-features" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:conclusion-features">37</a> with sufficiently small $\zeta$ that the feature replacement has iterated upper-limit error at most $\gamma/3$. Their degrees and coefficients are now fixed, and the approximants have the stated bounds depending only on the fixed coarse partition. Next choose a fixed $P_1$ by Lemma <a href="#lem:conclusion-periodic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:conclusion-periodic">38</a>. Replacing $\mathfrak S(|k-i|)$ by $\mathfrak S_{P_1}(k-i)$ in the energy costs at most $\gamma/3$ in upper limit. To check the normalization, the absolute error is bounded by a fixed constant times $$\frac1{MT}\sum_{i=1}^M
   \sum_{\substack{1\le k\le M\\0<|k-i|\le T}}
      |\mathfrak S(|k-i|)-\mathfrak S_{P_1}(k-i)|
 \le\frac2T\sum_{j=1}^T
      |\mathfrak S(j)-\mathfrak S_{P_1}(j)|.$$ The label and origin bounds have been absorbed in that fixed constant.

Choose a fixed $0<\delta<\eta/4$, to be made small after $P_1$ has been fixed. Partition $\{1,\ldots,M\}$ into a fixed number $R_0$ of consecutive blocks $J_a$, $1\le a\le R_0$, of lengths $L_a$ differing by at most one. Choosing $R_0$ sufficiently large in terms of $\delta,C_6$, for all sufficiently large $B$ we have $$c_{\delta,C_6}T\le L_a\le\delta T
 \qquad(1\le a\le R_0)$$ with a positive fixed lower constant. In particular all these lengths tend to infinity.

Call an ordered block pair fully allowed when every $(i,k)\in J_a\times
J_b$ satisfies $\eta T\le|k-i|\le T$. Discard any block pair containing both an allowed and a nonallowed pair of positions. The values of $|k-i|$ on a block pair vary by at most $2\delta T$. Thus each discarded allowed pair has lag within $2\delta T$ of $\eta T$ or $T$. For each row there are only $O(\delta T+1)$ such positions. The factors now present, including the periodic lag factor, are bounded for the fixed $P_1$ and coarse partition. With the normalization $1/(MT)$, this discarding costs $O(\delta)+O(1/T)$, with a constant allowed to depend on these already fixed parameters.

Choose a representative position $i_a\in J_a$ for every block, and for every fully allowed block pair put $\lambda_{ab}=|i_b-i_a|/T\in[\eta,1]$. Smoothness of $w$ on the fixed compact $s$-range and on $\eta\le\lambda\le1$ gives $$\sup_{s\in\mathcal I_s}
  |w(s,|k-i|/T)-w(s,\lambda_{ab})|\ll_\eta\delta
   \qquad(i\in J_a, k\in J_b).$$ Replacing the weight by this representative value therefore has another $O(\delta)$ cost. We fix $\delta$ sufficiently small that the two block errors together have upper limit at most $\gamma/3$. Neither the representatives nor their scaled lags have to converge as $B$ grows; only this uniform bound is used.

<a id="eq:conclusion-block-mean-square"></a>
It remains to show that the fully factored expression tends to zero. For $0\le r<D$ and $1\le l\le m_1$, define $$A_{a,r,l}(n)
   =\frac1{L_a}
       \sum_{\substack{i\in J_a\\i\equiv r\pmod D}}
          F_x(n+i)\widetilde V_{l,B}(S_i(n)).$$ By the feature identity, this is a fixed finite linear combination of $$\frac1{L_a}\sum_{i\in J_a}
       \mathbf 1_{i\equiv r\pmod D}F_x(n+i)G_{B,v}(n+i).$$ The lengths $L_a$ tend to infinity and the starting indices are fixed for each $B$. The modulus $D$, monomials $v$, and polynomial coefficients are all fixed before $B$ grows. Lemma <a href="#lem:labels-short" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:labels-short">5</a> applies with origins $n\in[s_0Tx,s_1Tx]$, and yields $$\lim_{B\to\infty}\ \limsup_{x\to\infty}
    \frac1{Tx}\sum_{n:\,n/(Tx)\in\mathcal I_s}
                   |A_{a,r,l}(n)|^2=0
 \label{eq:conclusion-block-mean-square}$$ for every one of the finitely many indices.

<a id="eq:conclusion-factorization"></a>
Set $c_{r,r'}=\mathfrak S_{P_1}(r'-r)$, using the periodic extension in Lemma <a href="#lem:conclusion-periodic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:conclusion-periodic">38</a>, including at the zero residue. On a block pair, splitting each position by its residue class gives the factored expression $$\frac{k_B}{Tx}
   \sum_{n:\,n/(Tx)\in\mathcal I_s}
   \sum_{l=1}^{m_1}h_l
   \sum_{(a,b)\ \mathrm{fully\ allowed}}
      \frac{L_aL_b}{MT}\,w(n/(Tx),\lambda_{ab})
      \sum_{r,r'=0}^{D-1}c_{r,r'}
            \overline{A_{a,r,l}(n)}A_{b,r',l}(n).
 \label{eq:conclusion-factorization}$$ The feature polynomials are real, so the conjugation here matches exactly the conjugation in the original energy. The factors $L_aL_b/(MT)$ are uniformly bounded. The numbers of cells, blocks, and residue classes are fixed, and $k_B$ and the representative smooth weights are uniformly bounded. Cauchy–Schwarz in the origin variable and <a href="#eq:conclusion-block-mean-square" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:conclusion-block-mean-square">[eq:conclusion-block-mean-square]</a> make every term in <a href="#eq:conclusion-factorization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:conclusion-factorization">[eq:conclusion-factorization]</a> tend to zero in the asserted iterated upper-limit sense. This remains true when the bounded coefficients depend on $n/(Tx)$ or on $B$.

The original main energy consequently has iterated upper limit at most $\gamma$ in absolute value. Since the approximation parameters were fixed before the limits and $\gamma>0$ was arbitrary, the proposition follows. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-conclusion-main-vanishes-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-conclusion-main-vanishes</summary>

*Proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:mixed" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:mixed">4</a>.* Suppose mixed decorrelation fails for some fixed $J$ and two fixed phase vectors. Fix the resulting subsequence, the limiting profile $W$ and bump $\phi$ from Section <a href="#sec:labels" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:labels">2</a>, and the smooth functions $\rho_0,\psi$ from Section <a href="#sec:amplification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:amplification">4</a>. Choose the regularity grid and tolerances as required for the estimates of Sections <a href="#sec:arithmetic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:arithmetic">3</a> and <a href="#sec:moments" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:moments">6</a>. The amplification then supplies the positive constant $c_5$ in <a href="#eq:15" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:15">[eq:15]</a>. This constant is independent of every sufficiently large fixed $C_*$.

We collect the upper bounds, recording the dependencies needed to choose parameters without a cycle. The diagonal contribution to $I_{2,x}/(BT)$ tends to zero in the prescribed order. Equations <a href="#eq:16" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:16">[eq:16]</a>–<a href="#eq:19" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:19">[eq:19]</a> express the remaining contribution as the block energy, up to errors bounded in iterated upper limit by $$C_0\eta+C_0/C_6.$$ Here $C_0$ can be chosen independently of $C_*$, $\eta$ and $C_6$, by the untruncated mean bound <a href="#eq:18" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:18">[eq:18]</a>.

The root comparison <a href="#eq:21" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:21">[eq:21]</a> and the sampling bound <a href="#eq:45" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:45">[eq:45]</a> allow replacement of this block kernel by $\mathcal M$. The resulting upper-limit error is bounded by $$C_1 e^{-c_3C_*}+C_{\eta,C_6}\epsilon_1+C_2\epsilon_2.$$ The constants $C_1,C_2$ do not depend on the later choices $\eta,C_6$ or the coarse partition. Indeed the truncation term in <a href="#eq:44" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:44">[eq:44]</a> has a coefficient independent of the lag range and block length, and converting a real cut-norm bound to an energy of labels of absolute value at most two costs only an absolute factor. For example, splitting each label into its real and imaginary parts gives a factor at most $16$. Integrating over $\mathcal I_s$ costs only its fixed length. The finite-residue counting passage and the uniformity in $s$ were established in Sections <a href="#sec:graph" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:graph">5</a> and <a href="#sec:sampling" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:sampling">9</a>. The remaining $o(1)$ errors vanish as $B\to\infty$ after the inner $x$-limit, with all the displayed parameters fixed.

<a id="eq:conclusion-final-upper"></a>
For any such fixed choices, Proposition <a href="#prop:conclusion-main-vanishes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:conclusion-main-vanishes">39</a> makes the main energy zero in upper limit. Consequently $$\limsup_{B\to\infty}\ \limsup_{x\to\infty}
       \frac{I_{2,x}}{BT}
 \le C_1e^{-c_3C_*}
      +C_0\eta+C_0/C_6
      +C_{\eta,C_6}\epsilon_1+C_2\epsilon_2.
 \label{eq:conclusion-final-upper}$$ First choose a sufficiently large fixed $C_*$ so that the first term is less than $c_5/10$. Next choose a sufficiently small fixed $\eta$ and a sufficiently large fixed $C_6$ so that the next two terms are each less than $c_5/10$. Then choose positive fixed $\epsilon_1,\epsilon_2$ so that their terms are each less than $c_5/10$, and choose the corresponding coarse partition in <a href="#eq:32" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:32">[eq:32]</a>. All feature, periodic and block approximations in Proposition <a href="#prop:conclusion-main-vanishes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:conclusion-main-vanishes">39</a> are made subsequently with these parameters fixed. Thus <a href="#eq:conclusion-final-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:conclusion-final-upper">[eq:conclusion-final-upper]</a> is strictly smaller than $c_5$, contradicting the lower bound <a href="#eq:15" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:15">[eq:15]</a>.

Finally, the bad subsequence was extracted from an arbitrary sequence of integer scales on which $x^{-1}\sum_{1\le n<x}\overline{g_x(n)}F_x(n+1)$ stays a fixed positive distance from zero. Every inner limit above uses the final subsequence selected for the profile. At each fixed $B$, all multipliers, residue moduli and shifts are finite, so the argument never requires their invariance or counting estimates while $B$ and $x$ grow simultaneously. The contradiction rules out every original sequence of this kind. Thus the mixed correlation tends to zero along the full sequence of integer scales, for every fixed pair of phase vectors. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:distribution"></a>
### Marginals, the joint law, and the ordering corollary

Proposition <a href="#prop:mixed" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:mixed">4</a> separates every pair of bin characters. Finite Fourier inversion therefore separates bin events, including smoothness at rational powers of the counting scale. The factorial-moment limits from Section <a href="#sec:labels" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:labels">2</a> identify their marginals with the Dickman law. We then obtain the fixed-scale joint law through every real counting endpoint, pass to the moving thresholds in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>, and deduce the comparison corollary from the continuous limiting distribution.

<a id="pm-h-59"></a>
#### Finite Fourier inversion

<a id="eq:distribution-characters"></a>
Adding back the centering in Proposition <a href="#prop:mixed" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:mixed">4</a> gives $$\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac1x\sum_{1\le n<x}
       \overline{g_x(n)}f_x(n+1)=\overline{\mu_g}\mu.
 \label{eq:distribution-characters}$$ Here and throughout this subsection, $x$ runs through positive integers. Indeed, the mean of $\overline{g_x(n)}$ tends to $\overline{\mu_g}$ by Lemma <a href="#lem:labels-marginal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:labels-marginal">3</a> and its initial-segment extension. For $m\le x$, write $$\boldsymbol b_x(m)
   =\bigl(\Omega_{\mathcal B_{k,x}}(m)\bigr)_{1\le k<J}.$$ Every coordinate lies in $\{0,\ldots,J\}$: each counted prime factor exceeds $x^{1/J}$. Thus reduction modulo $J+1$ is injective on the set of count vectors. Put $\zeta_0=\exp(2\pi i/(J+1))$. For $\boldsymbol r\in\{0,\ldots,J\}^{J-1}$ and such a count vector $\boldsymbol b$, the exact identity $$\mathbf 1_{\{\boldsymbol b=\boldsymbol r\}}
  =\frac1{(J+1)^{J-1}}
    \sum_{\boldsymbol h\in\{0,\ldots,J\}^{J-1}}
       \zeta_0^{\boldsymbol h\cdot(\boldsymbol b-\boldsymbol r)}$$ expresses each bin event as a finite linear combination of characters. The two phase vectors in <a href="#eq:distribution-characters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:distribution-characters">[eq:distribution-characters]</a> are independent choices, and complex conjugation simply permutes the available root-of-unity phases. Applying this identity at $n$ and $n+1$ proves factorization of the limiting expectations of every pair of fixed functions of their count vectors. Both marginal limits exist by Lemma <a href="#lem:labels-marginal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:labels-marginal">3</a>; the shift of the averaging range changes a bounded marginal average by $O(1/x)$.

If $c,d\in(0,1)$ are rational, choose $J$ so that both are grid points. For $m\le x$, the condition $P^+(m)\le x^c$ is exactly the vanishing of all counts in bins with lower endpoint at least $x^c$. Hence the joint smoothness event with thresholds $x^c,x^d$ has a limiting density equal to the product of its two marginal limits. The next calculation identifies those limits.

<a id="pm-h-60"></a>
#### The Dickman marginal

We recover the classical marginal law, originating with Dickman and developed by Ramaswami and de Bruijn (Dickman 1930; Ramaswami 1949; de Bruijn 1951) <a id="pm-cite-42"></a>[[Dickman1930](#ref-Dickman1930), [Ramaswami1949](#ref-Ramaswami1949), [deBruijn1951](#ref-deBruijn1951)], from the factorial moments already computed for the bin counts.

<a id="stmt-lem-distribution-dickman"></a>
<div id="lem:distribution-dickman" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:distribution-dickman"></a>
**Lemma 40** (Dickman marginal). *For every fixed $c\in(0,1)$, $$\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac1x
   \#\{1\le m\le x:P^+(m)\le x^c\}=\rho(1/c).
 \label{eq:distribution-dickman}$$ The limit is through all real $x$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-distribution-dickman"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-distribution-dickman</summary>

*Proof.* First suppose that $c=k_0/J$ for integers $J\ge2$ and $1\le k_0<J$. For $m\le x$, set $$N_{c,x}(m)=\sum_{k=k_0}^{J-1}\Omega_{\mathcal B_{k,x}}(m).$$ Then $P^+(m)\le x^c$ exactly when $N_{c,x}(m)=0$, and $0\le N_{c,x}(m)\le J$. For a nonnegative integer $z$, write $(z)_h=z(z-1)\cdots(z-h+1)$, with $(z)_0=1$. The finite inclusion–exclusion identity is $$\mathbf 1_{\{N_{c,x}(m)=0\}}
    =\sum_{h=0}^{J}\frac{(-1)^h}{h!}(N_{c,x}(m))_h.$$ For each $0\le h\le J$, the falling-factorial multinomial identity gives $$(N_{c,x}(m))_h
 =\sum_{\substack{r_{k_0},\ldots,r_{J-1}\ge0\\
                   r_{k_0}+\cdots+r_{J-1}=h}}
     \frac{h!}{\prod_{k=k_0}^{J-1}r_k!}
     \prod_{k=k_0}^{J-1}
       \bigl(\Omega_{\mathcal B_{k,x}}(m)\bigr)_{r_k}.$$ Apply the initial-segment form of <a href="#eq:labels-factorial-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:labels-factorial-limit">[eq:labels-factorial-limit]</a> to every term, with $r_k=0$ for $k<k_0$. The multinomial coefficient counts the assignments of the $h$ ordered variables to the bins. Summing the resulting simplex integrals therefore joins those bins into $(c,1]$ and gives $$\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac1x\sum_{1\le m\le x}(N_{c,x}(m))_h
 =\int_{\substack{s_1,\ldots,s_h\in(c,1]\\
                   s_1+\cdots+s_h\le1}}
       \prod_{i=1}^h\frac{ds_i}{s_i}.$$ For $h=0$, the empty integral is one, and the left-hand limit is also one. The initial-segment limit used here holds through real $x$, as does the factorial-moment limit in Section <a href="#sec:labels" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:labels">2</a>.

In this integral put $s_i=cv_i$. The upper bound on each $v_i$ is then implied by $v_1+\cdots+v_h\le1/c$, and changing the lower boundary from $v_i>1$ to $v_i\ge1$ does not change the integral. Averaging the inclusion–exclusion identity shows that the rational smoothness density is $H(1/c)$, where for $u\ge0$ we put <a id="eq:distribution-H"></a>$$H(u)=1+\sum_{h\ge1}\frac{(-1)^h}{h!}I_h(u),\qquad
 I_h(u)=\int_{\substack{v_1,\ldots,v_h\ge1\\
                         v_1+\cdots+v_h\le u}}
                  \prod_{i=1}^h\frac{dv_i}{v_i}.
 \label{eq:distribution-H}$$ The sum is locally finite because $I_h(u)=0$ when $h>u$. In particular, $1/c\le J$ ensures that the sum at $u=1/c$ agrees with the finite inclusion–exclusion sum above. Put $I_0(u)=1$ for $u\ge0$.

For $h\ge1$ and $y>z\ge1$, symmetry and the identity $1=(v_1+\cdots+v_h)/(v_1+\cdots+v_h)$ give <a id="eq:distribution-delay"></a>$$I_h(y)-I_h(z)
       =h\int_z^y I_{h-1}(t-1)\frac{dt}{t}.
 \label{eq:distribution-delay}$$ To verify this, cancel the last denominator after using symmetry and set $t=v_1+\cdots+v_h$. The remaining variables satisfy $v_1+\cdots+v_{h-1}\le t-1$. The same formula holds for $h=1$. Thus the $I_h$ are continuous, $H=1$ on $[0,1]$, and $$uH'(u)=-H(u-1)\qquad(u>1).$$ Successive integration on the intervals $[k,k+1]$ uniquely determines a continuous solution from its values on $[0,1]$. Hence $H=\rho$, proving <a href="#eq:distribution-dickman" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:distribution-dickman">[eq:distribution-dickman]</a> for rational $c$.

For an arbitrary real $c\in(0,1)$, choose rational $c_-<c<c_+$ in $(0,1)$. The smoothness event at $x^{c_-}$ is contained in the event at $x^c$, which is contained in the event at $x^{c_+}$. The rational limits bound the lower and upper limits of the middle normalized count by $\rho(1/c_-)$ and $\rho(1/c_+)$. Letting $c_-,c_+$ approach $c$ and using continuity of $\rho$ at the finite argument $1/c$ proves <a href="#eq:distribution-dickman" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:distribution-dickman">[eq:distribution-dickman]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:distribution-D"></a>
The marginal law also supplies the endpoint behavior of the limiting distribution. It gives $0\le\rho(u)\le1$ for $u>1$, and the same bounds hold at $u=1$ by definition. The delay equation makes $\rho$ nonincreasing on $[1,\infty)$. Its limit at infinity must be zero: if that limit were $L>0$, integrating $\rho'(u)=-\rho(u-1)/u\le-L/u$ would contradict nonnegativity. Consequently $$D(t)=
 \begin{cases}
  0,&t\le0,\\
  \rho(1/t),&0<t<1,\\
  1,&t\ge1
 \end{cases}
 \label{eq:distribution-D}$$ is a continuous distribution function, including at zero and one, and its probability measure has no atoms.

<a id="pm-h-61"></a>
#### Fixed and moving thresholds

<a id="eq:distribution-rational"></a>
<a id="eq:distribution-fixed"></a>
Combining the Fourier inversion with Lemma <a href="#lem:distribution-dickman" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:distribution-dickman">40</a> gives, for every pair of rational $c,d\in(0,1)$, $$\lim_{\substack{x\to\infty\\x\in\mathbb N}}\frac1x
 \#\{1\le n<x:P^+(n)\le x^c,\
                     P^+(n+1)\le x^d\}
     =\rho(1/c)\rho(1/d).
 \label{eq:distribution-rational}$$ We first extend this fixed-scale law to arbitrary real exponents and real counting endpoints. Fix $c,d\in(0,1)$ and rational exponents $c_-<c<c_+$ and $d_-<d<d_+$ in $(0,1)$. Given real $X$, put $x=\lfloor X\rfloor+1$. Then $x/X\to1$, the integer ranges $n\le X$ and $n<x$ agree, and for all sufficiently large $X$, $$x^{c_-}\le X^c\le x^{c_+},\qquad
 x^{d_-}\le X^d\le x^{d_+}.$$ Thus the normalized count over $1\le n\le X$ with thresholds $X^c,X^d$ lies between $x/X$ times the two normalized counts in <a href="#eq:distribution-rational" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:distribution-rational">[eq:distribution-rational]</a> at the lower and upper rational exponents. Taking lower and upper limits through real $X$, then letting the rational exponents approach $c,d$, proves $$\begin{split}
 &\lim_{X\to\infty}\frac1X
 \#\{2\le n\le X:P^+(n)\le X^c,\ P^+(n+1)\le X^d\}\\
 &\qquad=D(c)D(d)=\rho(1/c)\rho(1/d)
 \qquad(0<c,d<1).
 \end{split}
 \label{eq:distribution-fixed}$$ The omission of $n=1$ changes at most one summand. In <a href="#eq:distribution-fixed" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:distribution-fixed">[eq:distribution-fixed]</a>, the exponents are fixed and the limit is through all real $X$.

<a id="eq:distribution-upper-tail"></a>
This also gives the fixed-scale upper-tail independence law discussed by Erdős and Pomerance (Erdős and Pomerance 1978, 311) <a id="pm-cite-43"></a>[[ErdosPomerance1978](#ref-ErdosPomerance1978)]: $$\begin{split}
 &\lim_{X\to\infty}\frac1X
 \#\{2\le n\le X:P^+(n)>X^c,\ P^+(n+1)>X^d\}\\
 &\qquad=(1-D(c))(1-D(d))
 \qquad(0\le c,d\le1).
 \end{split}
 \label{eq:distribution-upper-tail}$$ For interior exponents this follows by inclusion–exclusion from <a href="#eq:distribution-fixed" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:distribution-fixed">[eq:distribution-fixed]</a> and the two marginal laws. The marginal for $n+1$ is the one in Lemma <a href="#lem:distribution-dickman" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:distribution-dickman">40</a>, since shifting its counting range changes only $O(1)$ terms. Replacing either strict comparison in <a href="#eq:distribution-upper-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:distribution-upper-tail">[eq:distribution-upper-tail]</a> by a weak one has the same limit. Indeed, for fixed $c>0$, equality $P^+(m)=X^c$ is impossible unless $X^c$ is a prime $p$; in that case every such $m$ is divisible by $p$, giving only $O(X^{1-c}+1)=o(X)$ possibilities for $m\le X+1$. At exponent zero, the upper-tail condition holds for every $m\ge2$, and equality can occur only at $m=1$. At exponent one, an upper-tail condition on $m\le X+1$ has only $O(1)$ possible integers. These observations give the endpoint cases of <a href="#eq:distribution-upper-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:distribution-upper-tail">[eq:distribution-upper-tail]</a> as well. Its right-hand side is continuous on $[0,1]^2$ by <a href="#eq:distribution-D" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:distribution-D">[eq:distribution-D]</a>.

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-distribution-dickman-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-distribution-dickman</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* Fix $a,b\in(0,1)$ and choose fixed exponents $c_-<a<c_+$ and $d_-<b<d_+$ in $(0,1)$. For each fixed $\varepsilon\in(0,1)$, all sufficiently large real $X$ satisfy, uniformly for $\varepsilon X\le n\le X$, $$X^{c_-}\le n^a\le X^{c_+},\qquad
 X^{d_-}\le n^b\le X^{d_+}.$$ On this interval, the fixed-scale event at the lower exponents is contained in the moving-threshold event of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>, which is contained in the fixed-scale event at the upper exponents. The discarded initial interval costs at most $\varepsilon+O(1/X)$ in normalized counting. The limit through all real $X$ in <a href="#eq:distribution-fixed" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:distribution-fixed">[eq:distribution-fixed]</a> therefore bounds the lower and upper limits of the desired count by the corresponding products at the lower and upper exponents, with that error. Let $\varepsilon\downarrow0$, then let the exponents approach $a,b$. Continuity of $D$ gives the claimed product through all real endpoints, with ordinary, unweighted counting. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-62"></a>
#### The ordering probability

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-distribution-dickman-3"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-distribution-dickman</summary>

*Proof of Corollary <a href="#cor:comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:comparison">2</a>.* For $n\ge2$, put $$U_n=\frac{\log P^+(n)}{\log n},\qquad
 V_n=\frac{\log P^+(n+1)}{\log n}.$$ The empirical probability law means uniform sampling from $2\le n\le\lfloor X\rfloor$; its normalization $1/(\lfloor X\rfloor-1)$ differs from $1/X$ by a factor tending to one. Recall the continuous distribution function $D$ from <a href="#eq:distribution-D" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:distribution-D">[eq:distribution-D]</a>.

Although $0\le U_n\le1$, the second coordinate can slightly exceed one. This overshoot vanishes and does not alter the limiting law: for every $n\ge N\ge2$, $$0\le V_n\le\frac{\log(n+1)}{\log n}
       \le1+\frac1{N\log N}.$$ Consequently the empirical laws are tight and every limiting law is supported on $[0,1]^2$. More explicitly, clamp $V_n$ to $\widetilde V_n=\min(V_n,1)$. For $0<a,b<1$, the joint distribution functions of $(U_n,\widetilde V_n)$ are exactly those of $(U_n,V_n)$, hence converge by Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> to $D(a)D(b)$. These interior rectangles determine the limiting probability law: their masses approach one as $a,b\uparrow1$, and finite differences give the masses of all interior grid cells. Approximating continuous functions on $[0,1]^2$ by such grids proves weak convergence to the product law with marginal distribution function $D$. Removing the clamping does not affect this convergence, by the displayed bound and the vanishing proportion of $n<N$.

Let $U,V$ be independent with distribution function $D$. Continuity gives $\mathbb P(U=V)=0$, and exchangeability of this limiting pair gives $$\mathbb P(U<V)=\mathbb P(V<U)=\frac12.$$ The boundary of the set $\{(u,v):u<v\}$ is the diagonal, which has zero product mass. Weak convergence therefore yields $$\lim_{X\to\infty}\frac1X
    \#\{2\le n\le X:U_n<V_n\}=\frac12.$$ Since both logarithmic sizes have the same positive denominator, $U_n<V_n$ is exactly $P^+(n)<P^+(n+1)$. Applying the same continuity-set argument to $v<u$ gives the reverse ordering limit as well. The symmetry used here belongs to the independent limiting law; no symmetry of the finite consecutive-integer pairs is assumed. ◻

</details>

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "The exact reindexing of two divisor representations. The coefficient identity makes the new endpoints differ by $j$, while fixed-multiplier invariance preserves their centered labels.",
      "figure_id": "fig:graph-reindexing",
      "pdf_page": 36,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:graph-reindexing",
          "exact_quote": "The exact reindexing of two divisor representations. The\ncoefficient identity makes the new endpoints differ by $j$, while\nfixed-multiplier invariance preserves their centered labels.",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[>=latex, every node/.style={font=\\small}]\n\\node (la) at (0,0) {$l_a=(am+1)/c$};\n\\node (lb) at (0,-1.5) {$l_b=(bm+1)/c$};\n\\node (np) at (5,0) {$n'=bl_a$};\n\\node (nj) at (5,-1.5) {$n'+j=al_b$};\n\\draw[->] (la) -- node[above] {$\\times b$} (np);\n\\draw[->] (lb) -- node[below] {$\\times a$} (nj);\n\\draw[->] (np) -- node[right] {$+j$} (nj);\n\\node at (2.5,-0.75) {$a-b=jc$};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A commutative diagram showing reindexing relations between divisor representations:\n- Top-left node: $l_a = (am + 1)/c$\n- Top-right node: $n' = bl_a$\n- Bottom-left node: $l_b = (bm + 1)/c$\n- Bottom-right node: $n' + j = al_b$\n- Center text between the rows: $a - b = jc$\n- Horizontal directed arrow pointing from $l_a = (am + 1)/c$ to $n' = bl_a$, labeled $\\times b$ above the arrow\n- Horizontal directed arrow pointing from $l_b = (bm + 1)/c$ to $n' + j = al_b$, labeled $\\times a$ below the arrow\n- Vertical directed arrow pointing downwards from $n' = bl_a$ to $n' + j = al_b$, labeled $+j$ to the right of the arrow"
    }
  ],
